Title: Global Quasi-strong Solutions to the Voigt Regularization of a Diffuse Interface Model for Incompressible Two-phase Flows with Bulk-surface Interaction

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.25578v1

Published Time: Thu, 27 Aug 2026 00:57:57 GMT

Number of Pages: 69

Markdown Content:
# Global Quasi-strong Solutions to the Voigt Regularization of a Diffuse Interface Model for Incompressible Two-phase Flows with Bulk-surface Interaction 

Patrik Knopf * † , Maoyin Lv ‡ , Hao Wu ‡ § 

Abstract 

We analyze a thermodynamically consistent diffuse interface model for incompressible two-phase viscous flows with unmatched densities in a smooth bounded domain Ω ⊂ Rd (d = 2 , 3) . This model consists of the Navier–Stokes–Voigt equations for the velocity field and a convective Cahn–Hilliard equation with a singular potential for the phase-field variable. The resulting hydrodynamic system is subject to a generalized Navier slip boundary condition for the velocity field and Cahn–Hilliard-type dynamic boundary conditions for the phase-field variable and the chemical potential. With the aid of the Voigt regularization, we establish the existence of global quasi-strong solutions that satisfy an energy equality. This is achieved through a combination of delicate approximation schemes and a semi-Galerkin method. 

Keywords : Two-phase flow, Navier–Stokes–Voigt system, Cahn–Hilliard equation, generalized Navier bound-ary condition, dynamic boundary condition, global quasi-strong solution. 

MSC 2020 : 35A01, 35K55, 35Q35, 76D05.

1arXiv:2608.25578v1 [math.AP] 26 Aug 2026 1 Introduction 

The mathematical description of multi-phase flows is a central topic in modern continuum fluid dynamics. Such flows arise in a wide range of natural and industrial processes, including biological systems, chemical engineering, and materials science, and involve the evolution of complex fluid interfaces. A widely used diffuse-interface model for incompressible binary mixtures of immiscible fluids with unmatched densities is the so-called AGG model, which was proposed by Abels, Garcke, and Grün in [6]: 

∂t(ρ(φ)v) + div( v ⊗ (ρ(φ)v + J)) − div(2 ν(φ)Dv) + ∇p = −div( ∇φ ⊗ ∇ φ) in QT , (1.1a) 

div v = 0 in QT , (1.1b) 

∂tφ + v · ∇ φ = ∆ μ in QT , (1.1c) 

μ = −∆φ + F ′(φ) in QT . (1.1d) Here, T ∈ (0 , +∞) is a given final time, Ω ⊂ Rd (d = 2 , 3) is a bounded domain with smooth boundary 

Γ := ∂Ω, and we write QT := Ω × (0 , T ). The letter n denotes the outward unit normal vector on ∂Ω, and ∂n

denotes the associated normal derivative on ∂Ω. The unknowns v : Ω ×(0 , T ) → Rd, φ : Ω ×(0 , T ) → [−1, 1] ,and p : Ω × (0 , T ) → R represent the volume-averaged velocity field, the order parameter (i.e., the difference 

φ = φ2 − φ1 of the volume fractions φ1 and φ2 of the two fluids), and the pressure of the fluid mixture, respectively. The flux term J, the density ρ, and the viscosity ν are given by 

J = − ρ1 − ρ2

2 ∇μ, ρ(φ) = ρ1

1 + φ

2 + ρ2

1 − φ

2 , ν(φ) = ν1

1 + φ

2 + ν2

1 − φ

2 , (1.2) where the positive constants ρ1, ρ2 and ν1, ν2 represent the homogeneous densities and viscosities of the two fluids, respectively. Without loss of generality, we assume that ρ1 > ρ 2 > 0. As ρ is linear in φ, we write 

ρ′ := ρ′(φ) = ρ1 − ρ2

2

for simplicity. Moreover, the symbol Dv = 12 (∇v + ( ∇v)⊤) denotes the symmetric gradient of v, where the superscript ⊤ denotes the transposition of the matrix. The nonlinear function F is the homogeneous free energy density, which is assumed to exhibit a double-well structure, and F ′ is its derivative. For the description of binary fluids, the physically most relevant choice is the Flory–Huggins potential given by 

Flog (r) = Θ2 [(1 + r)ln (1 + r) + (1 − r)ln (1 − r)] − Θ0

2 r2, for all r ∈ (−1, 1) .

Here, the constant parameters Θ and Θ0 are assumed to fulfill the condition 0 < Θ < Θ0, which ensures that 

Flog is double-well shaped. In the case of matched densities ρ1 = ρ2, the AGG model (1.1) reduces to the well-known “Model H”, which was originally proposed in [38] and later rigorously derived in [36]. Since then, it has been investigated in numerous works such as [1, 33] and the references therein. For the development of thermodynamically consistent diffuse interface models for multi-phase flows with unmatched densities, we refer to [6, 11, 49, 57, 58, 64]. They are based on different choices of the mean velocity of the mixture as well as further constitutive assumptions. Further discussions on the existing Navier–Stokes–Cahn–Hilliard models can be found in [63], where a unified framework was developed. In the literature, the AGG model has mostly been supplemented with the classical no-slip boundary condi-tion for v and homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions for φ and μ:

v = 0 on ΣT , (1.3a) 

∂nφ = 0 on ΣT , (1.3b) 2∂nμ = 0 on ΣT . (1.3c) The AGG model (1.1), subject to (1.3a)–(1.3c) and suitable initial conditions, has been extensively studied; we refer to [3–5, 10, 28–30] and the references therein. Regarding progress in its nonlocal variant and other generalizations, we refer to [7–9, 17, 18, 20, 39]. Although the AGG model (1.1) is a useful model for the study of two-phase flows with unmatched densities, it inherits certain limitations resulting from the no-slip boundary condition on the velocity field and the homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions imposed on the underlying convective Cahn–Hilliard subsystem: (L1) As discussed in [56], the no-slip boundary condition (1.3a) is often inadequate for the description of moving contact line phenomena. This is mainly because it neglects convective effects at the boundary and in its close vicinity. (L2) The boundary condition (1.3b) implies that the diffuse interface intersects the boundary Γ at a perfect angle of ninety degrees. However, in the context of two-phase flows, the contact angle is expected to deviate from ninety degrees and even evolve dynamically due to the motion of the mixture [56]. (L3) The boundary condition (1.3c) can be regarded as a no-flux boundary condition, as it implies that the normal mass flux J · n vanishes at the boundary. Therefore, the system (1.1) can only describe the situation in which the mass of both fluids in the bulk is conserved. However, any transfer of material between bulk and surface (which could, for instance, be caused by absorption or desorption processes or chemical reactions that take place at the boundary) cannot be described (see, e.g., [40]). To overcome the aforementioned limitations (L1) and (L2), a new class of boundary conditions based on Onsager’s principle of maximal energy dissipation was proposed in [56]: 

v · n = 0 , ∂nμ = 0 , ψ = φ on ΣT , (1.4a) 

2ν(ψ)[ Dv n ]τ + βvτ = G(ψ)∇τ ψ on ΣT , (1.4b) 

∂tψ + vτ · ∇ τ ψ = −γG(ψ) on ΣT , (1.4c) 

G(ψ) = −κ∆τ ψ + ε∂ nφ + G′(ψ) on ΣT . (1.4d) It involves a generalized Navier slip boundary condition on the tangential part of the velocity field (see (1.4b)) and a second-order (Allen–Cahn type) dynamic boundary condition on the phase field (see (1.4c)–(1.4d)). Here, the subscript τ denotes the tangential component of any vector field w, i.e., wτ = w − (w · n)n, ∇τ is the tan-gential gradient on Γ and ∆τ denotes the Laplace–Beltrami operator on Γ. The phenomenological parameters 

β, κ and γ are positive, and the function G represents a surface double-well potential. The “Model H” endowed with the boundary conditions (1.4) was first studied in [21], where the authors proved the existence of global weak solutions for the cases where both F and G are polynomial-like potentials, or F is a singular potential and 

G is a polynomial. Later, the existence of global weak solutions to (1.1) under (1.4) was established in [23]. To overcome the limitation (L3) of the AGG model (1.1), a new class of boundary conditions was derived in [31]. It consists of Cahn–Hilliard-type dynamic boundary conditions for the phase-field variable φ and the chemical potential μ, and a generalized Navier slip boundary condition for the velocity field v:

v · n = 0 on ΣT , (1.5a)

L = −∆τ ψ + ∂nφ + G′(ψ) on ΣT . (1.5e) Here the positive slip parameter β may also depend on the surface phase-field ψ. The derivation of system (1.1) subject to the boundary conditions (1.5) was carried out in [31] by means of local mass balance laws, local energy dissipation laws, and the Lagrange multiplier approach. This model allows for surface diffusion, a dynamic contact angle between the bulk diffuse interface and the solid boundary, and possible mass transfer between bulk and surface. Given the initial conditions 

v|t=0 = v0, φ|t=0 = φ0 in Ω, ψ|t=0 = ψ0 = tr( φ0) on Γ, (1.6) the system (1.1) subject to (1.5)–(1.6) has been studied in [24, 31]. In [31], the authors considered the case 

L = 0 with regular potentials and matched densities, i.e., ρ1 = ρ2. They showed the existence of global weak solutions in two and three dimensions, as well as the uniqueness of the weak solution in two dimensions. Later, in [24], the existence of global weak solutions in two and three dimensions was established for singular potentials and unmatched densities when L ∈ (0 , +∞] or matched densities when L = 0 . For prescribed velocity fields in the bulk and on the boundary, the associated convective bulk-surface Cahn–Hilliard subsystem was analyzed in [32, 43–45]. The non-convective version (i.e., v ≡ 0) has been the subject of extensive investigation in the literature. We refer to [15, 19, 26, 27, 35, 40, 48, 50–52, 60] for a selection of representative contributions. For a comprehensive overview of the Cahn–Hilliard equation with classical or dynamic boundary conditions, we refer to the book [53] and the recent review paper [66]. Finally, we mention a new diffuse interface model for incompressible, viscous fluid mixtures with bulk-surface interaction, consisting of a Navier– Stokes–Cahn–Hilliard model in the bulk that is coupled to a surface Navier–Stokes–Cahn–Hilliard model on the boundary, which has been proposed and analyzed in [46, 61, 62]. Regarding the regularity of solutions to (1.1) subject to (1.5)–(1.6), it seems difficult to go beyond the mere existence of global weak solutions in the case of unmatched densities. This is due to the low regularity of the trace of the velocity field on the boundary and the fact that div Γvτ̸ = 0 on ΣT , which make the nonlinear coupling term div Γ(ψvτ ) difficult to handle. Instead, we shall consider an alternative new class of boundary conditions, which was recently derived in [42] based on local mass balance laws, local energy dissipation laws, and the Lagrange multiplier approach: 

v · n = 0 , div Γ vτ = 0 on ΣT , (1.7a) 

(

K∂ nφ = ψ − φ, if K ∈ [0 , +∞)

∂nφ = 0 , if K = + ∞ on ΣT , (1.7b)

L = −∆τ ψ + ∂nφ + G′(ψ) on ΣT . (1.7f) In particular, compared to (1.5b), the boundary condition (1.7c) involves an additional surface pressure q, which acts as a Lagrange multiplier for the condition div Γ vτ = 0 on ΣT . To our knowledge, no theoretical results are available for the AGG model (1.1) subject to the initial conditions (1.6) and boundary conditions (1.7). However, a strategy similar to that employed in [24] can be used to establish the existence of a global weak solution. In the case of unmatched densities, it remains difficult to obtain results beyond the existence of weak solutions. Nevertheless, inspired by [22], we will show that the existence of more regular solutions can at least 4be established for a Voigt regularization of the system (1.1), subject to (1.6)–(1.7). From a physical point of view, the Voigt regularization (sometimes also referred to as the Kelvin–Voigt regularization) is mainly used to describe viscoelastic fluids or highly turbulent flows. For mathematical purposes, it has also been extensively employed as a regularization of various incompressible flow models for analytical or numerical purposes. The Voigt-regularized stress tensor is given by 

T(v) = 2 ν(φ)Dv + 2 αD∂tv.

The additional term 2αD∂tv with α > 0 can be interpreted as a time relaxation (see, e.g., [13, 47, 55] and references therein). In summary, for any α > 0, we consider the following Voigt-regularized variant of the Navier–Stokes– Cahn–Hilliard model (1.1) subject to (1.6)–(1.7):

Goals and main ideas. The goal of this work is to establish the global existence of a more regular (with respect to time) solution to the system (1.8)–(1.10) (see Theorem 2.1). This so-called quasi-strong solution satisfies the energy equality in both two and three dimensions. The proof is based on an appropriate semi-Galerkin approximation scheme. In the following, we outline some features of the problem and key ideas of the proof. (1) Voigt regularization. The parameter α > 0 plays a significant role in the analysis, as the Voigt term 

div(2 αD∂tv) provides estimates for ∂tv in H1(Ω) . This enables us to improve the regularity of ∂tv and to show the existence of a quasi-strong solution. More precisely, to obtain the H1(Ω) -regularity of ∂tv,we need to take w = ∂tv as a test function for the velocity equation (cf. (2.13)). Then, there will emerge three integrals over the boundary Γ involving ∂tv, that is, 

Z

> Γ

β(ψ)vτ · ∂tvτ dS +

Z

> Γ

ψ∇τ L · ∂tvτ dS − 12

Z

> Γ

(J · n)vτ · ∂tvτ dS. 

Since α > 0, the term ∥∂tv∥L4(Γ) can be estimated using the Sobolev embedding theorem and eventually be controlled by Z

> Ω

ρ(φ)|∂tv|2 dx + 2 α

Z

> Ω

|D∂tv|2 dx. 

For more details, we refer to the terms K2, K4 and K11 in (4.36). (2) Construction of approximate solutions. We employ a suitable approximation procedure involving a vis-cous regularization to the bulk-surface convective Cahn–Hilliard subsystem (see Appendix A), together with an appropriate regularization of the initial data (see Proposition 3.1). Such approximations are cru-cial to rigorously justify higher-order Sobolev estimates for the corresponding approximate solutions. The most crucial step is to obtain the regularity 

(∂tμ, ∂ tL) ∈ L2(0 , T ; H1),

which allows us to rigorously compute a priori estimates. However, to derive this regularity prop-erty, we need some higher-order regularity of ∂nφ. When K ∈ (0 , +∞], this can be derived via the 6Robin/Neumann boundary condition K∂ nφ = ψ − φ on ΣT . Therefore, in Appendix A, we shall focus on the case 

(K, L ) ∈ (0 , +∞] × (0 , +∞),

and establish the existence of approximate solutions in this case (see Section 3). Then, by deriving suit-able estimates that are uniform with respect to the approximate parameters, we can deduce the existence of a global quasi-strong solution to problem (1.8)–(1.10) with (K, L ) ∈ (0 , +∞]×(0 , +∞) using a com-pactness argument (see Section 4). For the limit models corresponding to (K, L ) ∈ (0 , +∞] × { 0, +∞} ,the existence of a global quasi-strong solution can be obtained by studying the corresponding asymptotic limits as L → 0 and L → +∞ (see Section 5). (3) Motivation for an additional singular potential. For our analysis of the viscous approximation to the bulk-surface convective Cahn–Hilliard system in Appendix A, we introduce a further singular potential 

Fσ (see (3.5)), which exhibits its singularities at ±(1 − σ). It enforces a so-called strict separation property, which means that the values of the phase-field variables φ and ψ cannot exceed the interval 

(−1 + σ, 1 − σ). As the singularities of the potentials F and G lie at ±1, these potentials and their derivatives f = F ′ and g = G′ can be interpreted as regular functions on (−1 + σ, 1 − σ).In Appendix A, we prove the aforementioned strict separation property using a comparison argument. To this end, we rewrite the convective viscous Cahn–Hilliard system (A.1) as (A.24) and consider the ODE system (A.25). Taking the difference between (A.24) and (A.25) and testing the resultant with a suitable test function, we arrive at (A.27). By the monotonicity of fσ = F ′

> σ

, the terms in the second line of (A.27) are nonnegative. If we had not introduced the additional function fσ, we would have obtained the following ODE system instead of (A.25):

Here, f0 and g0 denote the singular parts of the functions f = F ′ and g = G′, respectively (see as-sumption (A2) ). Thus, the introduction of the singular potential fσ allows us to avoid the additional assumption f0 = g0.

Plan of this paper. In Section 2, we present some preliminaries for the analysis and state the main result of this paper. Sections 3 and 4 are devoted to the proof of Theorem 2.1 for the case (K, L ) ∈ (0 , +∞] × (0 , +∞).More precisely, the existence of approximate solutions is established in Section 3, while the existence of quasi-strong solutions to system (1.8)–(1.10) with (K, L ) ∈ (0 , +∞] × (0 , +∞) is shown in Section 4. In Section 5, we complete the proof of Theorem 2.1 by investigating the asymptotic limits as L → 0 and L → +∞ with 

K ∈ (0 , +∞]. Finally, in Appendix A, we establish the well-posedness of the bulk-surface convective Cahn– Hilliard subsystem with viscous regularization. 

# 2 Preliminaries and Main Result 

2.1 Preliminaries 

We write a ⊗ b = ( aibj )di,j =1 for any vectors a, b ∈ Rd and Asym = 12 (A + A⊤) for any matrix A ∈ Rd×d.Let X be a real Banach space. We denote its norm by ∥ · ∥ X , its dual space by X′ and the duality pairing by 7⟨· , ·⟩ X′,X . If X is a Hilbert space, its inner product will be denoted by (·, ·)X . The notation X , → Y means that X is continuously embedded in Y . We further denote by X the generic space of vectors or matrices, with each component belonging to X. The space Lq(0 , T ; X) with 1 ≤ q ≤ +∞ denotes the Bochner space consisting of all strongly measurable q-integrable functions with values in X, or, if q = + ∞, essentially bounded functions. Besides, the space C([0 , T ]; X) denotes the Banach space of all bounded and continuous functions u : [0 , T ] → X equipped with the supremum norm. For q ∈ [1 , +∞], W 1,q (0 , T ; X) denotes the space of functions f such that f ∈ Lq(0 , T ; X) with ∂tf ∈ Lq(0 , T ; X), where ∂t denotes the vector-valued distributional derivative of f with respect to t. For q = 2 , we set H1(0 , T ; X) = W 1,2(0 , T ; X).Let Ω ⊂ Rd (d ∈ { 2, 3}) be a bounded domain with smooth boundary Γ = ∂Ω. The Lebesgue spaces in Ω

and on Γ are denoted by Lq(Ω) , Lq(Γ) (1 ≤ q ≤ +∞) with norms ∥ · ∥ Lq (Ω) and ∥ · ∥ Lq (Γ) , respectively. For 

s ≥ 0 and q ∈ [1 , +∞), we denote by Hs,q (Ω) the Bessel-potential spaces and by W s,q (Ω) the Slobodeckij spaces. It holds Hs, 2(Ω) = W s, 2(Ω) for all s, but for q̸ = 2 the identity Hs,q (Ω) = W s,q (Ω) is only true if 

s ∈ N. For clarity, we denote by N the set of natural numbers including zero. If s ∈ N, then Hs,q (Ω) and 

W s,q (Ω) coincide with the usual Sobolev spaces. The corresponding function spaces over the boundary Γ are defined via local charts. Let Υi : Ui ⊂ Rd−1 → Γ be a finite family of parametrizations such that S 

> i

Υi(Ui)

The properties of the spaces over Ω described above can easily be transferred to the spaces over Γ.If q = 2 , Hs, 2(Ω) = W s, 2(Ω) for all s and these spaces are Hilbert spaces. Throughout this paper, we use the notation Hs(Ω) = Hs, 2(Ω) = W s, 2(Ω) and H0(Ω) is identified with L2(Ω) . The Lebesgue spaces, Sobolev spaces and Slobodeckij spaces on the boundary Γ can be defined analogously, provided that Γ is sufficiently regular. 2.2 Main result 

Let us introduce some assumptions that are used in the subsequent analysis. 

(A1) The coefficients satisfy ν, β ∈ C0,1loc (R) and 

0 < ν ∗ ≤ ν(r) ≤ ν∗, 0 < β ∗ ≤ β(r) ≤ β∗ for all r ∈ R.13 (A2) The bulk free energy density is given by 

F = F0 + F1,

where F0 ∈ C([ −1, 1]) ∩ C2(−1, 1) with F0(0) = 0 . For simplicity, we extend F0 by defining F0(r) = +∞ for all r ∈ R \ [−1, 1] . For the derivative f0 := F ′

(L − μ)vτ · wτ dS (cf. [24, 46]), while for L = 0 (with ρ1 = ρ2) or L = + ∞, this term simply vanish. Moreover, it follows from the regularities in Definition 2.1 that 

v ∈ H1(0 , T ; H1div (Ω)) ,→ C([0 , T ]; H1div (Ω)) .

As d ∈ { 2, 3}, the Aubin–Lions–Simon lemma, together with the Sobolev embedding theorem, gives 

φ ∈ C([0 , T ]; H2−ϵ) ,→ C([0 , T ]; C(Ω) × C(Γ)) 

for every ϵ ∈ (0 , 1/2) . Therefore, the initial conditions in (2.15) make sense. We are now in a position to state the main result of this work. 

Theorem 2.1 (Existence of global quasi-strong solutions) . Let Ω ⊂ Rd with d ∈ { 2, 3} be a bounded domain with smooth boundary Γ = ∂Ω and let T > 0 be a prescribed final time. Fix K ∈ (0 , +∞] and L ∈ [0 , +∞].Assume that (A1) –(A3) hold and let (v0, v0,τ ) ∈ H10,div and φ0 = ( φ0, ψ 0) ∈ W 2 

> K

be prescribed initial data with ∥φ0∥L∞ ≤ 1 and 

(

|m(φ0)| < 1, if L ∈ [0 , +∞),

|⟨ φ0⟩Ω| < 1, |⟨ ψ0⟩Γ| < 1, if L = + ∞. (2.16) 

Moreover, assume that the following additional assumption holds: 

(A4) (−∆φ0 + f0(φ0), −∆τ ψ0 + ∂nφ0 + g0(ψ0)) ∈ H 1.

In the case L = 0 , we further require ρ1 = ρ2 = const . > 0 and assume the compatibility condition: 

(C) −∆φ0 + f (φ0) = −∆τ ψ0 + ∂nφ0 + g(ψ0) a.e. on Γ.Then problem (1.8) –(1.10) admits a quasi-strong solution (v, φ, μ) on [0 , T ] in the sense of Definition 2.1. Furthermore, the energy identity

15 Remark 2.2. For (K, L ) ∈ (0 , +∞] × (0 , +∞), the assertion of the theorem will be established in Sec-tion 4. The proof is based on an approximation scheme presented in Section 3. In the limiting cases (K, L ) ∈

(0 , +∞] × { 0} and (K, L ) ∈ (0 , +∞] × { +∞} , the conclusion will be verified in Section 5. We note that the case (K, L ) ∈ { 0} × [0 , +∞] is not covered in the present work. One possible approach to establish the existence of global quasi-strong solutions for (K, L ) ∈ { 0} × [0 , +∞] is to pass to the limit as K → 0 with 

L fixed. In this case, however, the initial datum have to satisfy φ0 ∈ W 20 = V2. At present, it is unclear how to approximate such an initial datum by a sequence satisfying the assumptions of Theorem A.1. Hence, the approximation scheme developed in Section 3 cannot be directly applied to construct approximate solutions for the case K = 0 .

Remark 2.3. The matched-density assumption, i.e., ρ1 = ρ2 > 0, is required in our approach to the limit as L → 0. In the unmatched-density case, the lack of a uniform bound for ∥μL∥L2(0 ,T ;H2) with respect to 

L ∈ (0 , 1] (see Remark 5.1) prevents us from passing to the limit in the boundary term ρ′ R

> Γ

(∇μL·n)vL 

> τ

·wτ dS.By contrast, under the matched-density assumption, the density is constant and hence ρ′ ≡ 0. Therefore, the above boundary term vanishes identically, thereby avoiding this difficulty. In contrast to the case L → 0,no matched-density assumption is required when passing to the limit as L → +∞. Indeed, the uniform 

L∞(0 , T ; H1)-estimate for μL with respect to L ≥ L0 (cf. (5.17)), together with the trace theorem and the boundary condition, yields 

∥∂nμL∥L∞(0 ,T ;HΓ) = 1

L ∥L L − μL∥L∞(0 ,T ;HΓ) ≤ CL → 0, as L → +∞.

This, together with the uniform L∞(0 , T ; H1div (Ω)) -estimate for vL (cf. (4.4)), allows us to pass to the limit in the boundary term. 

Remark 2.4. The assumption (v0, v0,τ ) ∈ H10,div is a technical requirement that arises from our proof and is needed to derive uniform estimates of approximate solutions. More precisely, we introduce an approximate system whose solvability is established via a semi-Galerkin approximation scheme combined with a fixed-point argument inspired by [30]. In the semi-Galerkin scheme, the initial datum for the approximate velocity field 

vm is chosen as vm(0) = PΩ

> m

(v0, v0,τ ); see Section 3.2 for the definition of the projection operator Pm.To derive estimates that are uniform with respect to the Galerkin parameter m ∈ N, we need to control the term α R

3.3 Existence of approximate solutions 

We establish the existence of approximate solutions to problem (3.26)–(3.28) on [0 , T ] by a fixed-point argument (cf. [30]). To this aim, we fix (v, vτ ) ∈ H1(0 , T ; Vm) and consider the following convective viscous Cahn–Hilliard equation with dynamic boundary conditions 

∂tφm + v · ∇ φm = ∆ μm a.e. in QT , (3.29a) 

μm = γ∂ tφm − ∆φm + f (φm) + k−1fσ(φm) a.e. in QT , (3.29b) 

(

Lm = γ∂ tψm − ∆τ ψm + ∂nφm + g(ψm) + k−1fσ(ψm) a.e. on ΣT , (3.29f) subject to the initial conditions 

(φm, ψ m)|t=0 = ( φ0,σ,k , ψ 0,σ,k ) in Ω × Γ. (3.30) 22 According to Theorem A.1, there exists a unique solution (φm, μm) to (3.29)–(3.30) satisfying

# 4 Existence of Quasi-strong Solutions in the Case (K, L ) ∈ (0 , +∞] × (0 , +∞)

In this section, we establish our main result Theorem 2.1 in the case (K, L ) ∈ (0 , +∞] × (0 , +∞). To this end, we assume that the premises of Theorem 2.1 hold and we take 

m, k ∈ N+, 0 < σ ≤ c1

2(1 + c1) < 12 , 0 < γ < 1 (4.1) for a constant c1 > 0 (independent of m, k, σ and γ), which will be specified later in (4.11). We first derive uniform a priori estimates for the approximate solutions (vm, φm, μm) obtained in Sec-tion 3. Then, by means of compactness arguments, we pass to the limits as m → +∞, σ → 0, γ = k−1 → 0

to conclude the existence of a global quasi-strong solution [0 , T ].

4.1 Uniform estimates for the approximate solutions 

First of all, we have the following mass conservation law.

∥f0(φm)∥L∞(0 ,T ;H) + ∥g0(ψm)∥L∞(0 ,T ;HΓ) ≤ C, (4.45) 

k−1∥fσ(φm)∥L∞(0 ,T ;H) + k−1∥fσ(ψm)∥L∞(0 ,T ;HΓ) ≤ C, (4.46) 

∥φm∥L∞(0 ,T ;H2) ≤ C, (4.47) 

∥μm∥L2(0 ,T ;H2) ≤ C. (4.48) 

Furthermore, the following strict separation property holds 

φm ∈ L∞(QT ) such that |φm(x, t )| ≤ (1 − σ)(1 − eδ) a.e. in QT , (4.49) 

ψm ∈ L∞(Σ T ) such that |ψm(x, t )| ≤ (1 − σ)(1 − eδ) a.e. on ΣT , (4.50) 

for some eδ = eδ(σ, k, γ ) ∈ (0 , 1) .

Proof. Based on Lemma 4.6, the uniform bounds (4.45)–(4.47) can be shown in a similar way as in the proof of Lemmas 4.4 and 4.5. Furthermore, applying the regularity theory for bulk-surface elliptic problems (see, e.g., [41, Theorem 3.3]) to the subsystem (3.29a), (3.29d), (3.29a), and using Lemmas 4.2, 4.3 and 4.6, we can derive (4.48). Finally, combining Lemma 4.6 with Theorem A.1, we find that the strict separation property in (3.31) depends on σ, γ and k, but is independent of m. This yields (4.49) and (4.50). The proof is complete. 

4.2 Passage to the limit and existence of quasi-strong solutions 

In this subsection, we prove the existence of quasi-strong solutions in the case (K, L ) ∈ (0 , +∞]×(0 , +∞).

Proof of Theorem 2.1: (K, L ) ∈ (0 , +∞] × (0 , +∞). We will prove the assertions by choosing γ := k−1

and passing to the limits as m → +∞, σ → 0 and k → +∞ in sequence. 

Passage to the limit as m → +∞. Based on the uniform estimates established in Lemmas 4.2, 4.6 and 4.7, we apply the Banach–Alaoglu theorem and the Aubin–Lions–Simon lemma to conclude, up to subsequence extraction, the following convergences as m → +∞:

vm → vσ weakly star in L∞(0 , T ; H1div (Ω)) , (4.51a) 

∂tvm → ∂tvσ weakly in L2(0 , T ; H1div (Ω)) , (4.51b) 

(vm, vm, τ ) → (vσ, vσ, τ ) strongly in C([0 , T ]; L2div ), (4.51c) 

φm → φσ weakly star in L∞(0 , T ; H2) and strongly in C([0 , T ]; W1,p ), (4.51d) 

∂tφm → ∂tφσ weakly in L2(0 , T ; H1), (4.51e) 

μm → μσ weakly star in L∞(0 , T ; H1) and weakly in L2(0 , T ; H2), (4.51f) for all p ∈ [2 , 6) . Like before, here we use the notation φσ = ( φσ, ψ σ) and μσ = ( μσ, Lσ). As a consequence of the above convergences, we infer that 

ρ(φm) → ρ(φσ) strongly in C([0 , T ]; W 1,p (Ω)) ,ν(φm) → ν(φσ) strongly in C([0 , T ]; W 1,p (Ω)) ,β(ψm) → β(ψσ) strongly in C([0 , T ]; W 1,p (Γ)) ,

for all p ∈ [2 , 6) . Moreover, due to Lemma 4.7, we have 

φσ ∈ L∞(QT ) such that |φσ(x, t )| ≤ (1 − σ)(1 − eδ) a.e. in QT , (4.52a) 43 ψσ ∈ L∞(Σ T ) such that |ψσ(x, t )| ≤ (1 − σ)(1 − eδ) a.e. on ΣT , (4.52b) with eδ = eδ(σ, k, γ ) ∈ (0 , 1) .The above properties guarantee the convergence of the nonlinear terms in (3.26), and of the singular non-linearities k−1fσ(φm) in (3.27b) and k−1fσ(ψm) in (3.27f). This allows us to pass to the limit as m → +∞ in (3.26)–(3.27), which entails that the limit functions (vσ, vσ, τ , φσ, μσ) satisfy the weak formulation

Passage to the limit as σ → 0. As norms on Banach spaces are weakly (star) lower semicontinuous, we infer from the convergences (4.51) and the uniform bounds established in Lemmas 4.2, 4.6 and 4.7 that 

∥vσ∥L∞(0 ,T ;H1div (Ω)) + ∥μσ∥L∞(0 ,T ;H1) + ∥φσ∥L∞(0 ,T ;H2) + √γ∥∂tφσ∥L∞(0 ,T ;L2) ≤ C, (4.56a)

# 5 Existence of Quasi-strong Solutions to the Limit Models 

To establish Theorem 2.1 in the limiting cases (K, L ) ∈ (0 , ∞] × { 0, +∞} , we investigate the asymptotic limits as L → 0 and L → +∞, respectively, when K ∈ (0 , +∞].47 5.1 The case (K, L ) ∈ (0 , +∞] × { 0}

In this subsection, we restrict ourselves to the matched-density case, i.e., ρ1 = ρ2 > 0. By examining the proof of Theorem 2.1 in the case (K, L ) ∈ (0 , +∞] × (0 , +∞) more closely, we observe that the estimates (4.16), (4.43) may depend on L ∈ (0 , 1] . The following two lemmas provide refined versions of the uniform estimates derived in Section 4, where the estimates are now independent of L ∈ (0 , 1] .

Proof. Based on Lemmas 5.3 and 5.4, we can repeat the proof of Lemmas 4.6 and 4.7 to verify the claimed estimates. In particular, the constant in the estimate (4.43) can now be chosen independently of L ∈ [L0, +∞).The proof is complete. In view of Lemmas 5.3, 5.4 and 5.5, we are now ready to handle the case (K, L ) ∈ (0 , +∞] × { +∞} .

Proof of Theorem 2.1: (K, L ) ∈ (0 , +∞] × { +∞} . Based on the refined estimates in Lemmas 5.3, 5.4 and 5.5, we can repeat the argument in Section 4.2 to conclude that there exist functions (vL, vL 

> τ

, φL, μL) satisfying the weak formulation (5.6) and the equations in (5.7). As norms on Banach spaces are weakly (star) lower semicontinuous, and the constants C in Lemmas 5.3, 5.4 and 5.5 are independent of the parameters m, σ,

γ = k−1, and L ∈ [L0, +∞), we can repeat the argument in Section 4.2 by passing to the limit as m → +∞,

σ → 0, γ = k−1 → 0 subsequently, and conclude that the uniform estimate (5.9) holds for all L ∈ [L0, +∞).Then, similar to the process for the case L = 0 , we can prove Theorem 2.1 in the current case (K, L ) ∈

(0 , +∞]×{ +∞} by passing to the limit as L → +∞ in (5.6) and (5.7). The details are omitted for brevity. 54 A Analysis of a Bulk-Surface Convective Viscous Cahn–Hilliard System 

Let γ > 0, σ ∈ (0 , 1/2) , k ∈ N+. We consider the following bulk-surface convective viscous Cahn– Hilliard system 

∂tφ + v · ∇ φ = ∆ μ in QT , (A.1a) 

μ = γ∂ tφ − ∆φ + f (φ) + k−1fσ(φ) in QT , (A.1b) 

(

K∂ nφ = ψ − φ, if K ∈ (0 , +∞)

∂nφ = 0 , if K = + ∞ on ΣT , (A.1c) 

L∂ nμ = L − μ, L ∈ (0 , +∞) on ΣT , (A.1d) 

∂tψ + vτ · ∇ τ ψ = ∆ τ L − ∂nμ on ΣT , (A.1e) 

L = γ∂ tψ − ∆τ ψ + ∂nφ + g(ψ) + k−1fσ(ψ) on ΣT , (A.1f) subject to the initial conditions 

φ(0) = φ0 in Ω, ψ(0) = ψ0 on Γ. (A.2) Here, v is a prescribed velocity field and fσ is the nonlinear function introduced in (3.1). In the following, we establish the well-posedness and regularity results for problem (A.1)–(A.2). The anal-ysis extends the previous work [30] to the viscous approximation of a bulk-surface convective Cahn–Hilliard system. 

Theorem A.1. Let Ω ⊂ Rd with d ∈ { 2, 3} be a bounded domain with smooth boundary Γ. Assume that 

(A2) and (A3) hold, γ > 0, σ ∈ (0 , 1/2) , k ∈ N+. Moreover, we assume that v is a velocity field satisfying 

(v, vτ ) ∈ L∞(0 , T ; L2div ∩ L3), and the initial data φ0 = ( φ0, ψ 0) ∈ H 1 ∩ L ∞ satisfies ∥φ0∥L∞ ≤ 1 − σ

and |m(φ0)| < 1 − σ. Then, problem (A.1) –(A.2) admits a unique weak solution (φ, μ) on [0 , T ] such that 



φ = ( φ, ψ ) ∈ L∞(0 , T ; H1) ∩ L2(0 , T ; H2), (∂tφ, ∂ tψ) ∈ L2(0 , T ; L2),

|φ(x, t )| < 1 − σ a.e. in QT , |ψ(x, t )| < 1 − σ a.e. on ΣT ,

μ = ( μ, L) ∈ L2(0 , T ; H2),  f0(φ), g 0(ψ) ∈ L2(0 , T ; L2),

(A.3) 

which satisfies the equations in (A.1) almost everywhere in QT (resp. ΣT ) and the initial conditions (A.2) 

almost everywhere in Ω (resp. Γ). In addition, the weak solution has the following regularity properties:

(

Proof. The proof is divided into several steps. When K = + ∞, the boundary condition of φ reduces to the homogeneous Neumann boundary condition ∂nφ = 0 on ΣT . This case can be treated by a limiting procedure as K → +∞, so we focus on the case K ∈ (0 , +∞) below. 

Existence. The existence of a global weak solution on [0 , T ] with the regularity (A.3) can be shown in a stan-dard way: first for regular potentials through a Faedo–Galerkin method (cf. [44]), and afterwards for singular potentials by means of a Yosida approximation (cf. [45]). 

Uniqueness. For i = 1 , 2, let φ0,i = ( φ0,i , ψ 0,i ) be two initial data, which are admissible in the sense of Theorem A.1 and satisfy m(φ0,1) = m(φ0,2). Furthermore, let (φi, μi) with φi = ( φi, ψ i) and μi = ( μi, Li)

be the corresponding weak solutions of problem (A.1)–(A.2). For brevity, we use the notation 

eφ = ( eφ, eψ) := φ1 − φ2, eμ = ( eμ, eL) := μ1 − μ2, eφ0 = ( eφ0, eψ0) := φ0,1 − φ0,2.

(A.7) and

eL = γ∂ t eψ − ∆τ eψ + ∂n eφ + g(ψ1) − g(ψ2) + k−1fσ(ψ1) − k−1fσ(ψ2) a.e. on ΣT .

Regularity (R2) . We employ a strategy that has been applied to the viscous (bulk) Cahn–Hilliard equation with classical Neumann boundary conditions in [54, Proposition A.3] and [30, Theorem A.1]. To this end, we rewrite the subsystem (A.1b), (A.1c), (A.1f) as 

γ∂ tφ − ∆φ + k−1fσ(φ) = h a.e. in QT , (A.24a) 

K∂ nφ = ψ − φ a.e. on ΣT , (A.24b) 

γ∂ tψ − ∆τ ψ + ∂nφ + k−1fσ(ψ) = hΓ a.e. on ΣT , (A.24c) 61 where 

h := μ − f (φ), hΓ := L − g(ψ).

Due to (A.20), (A.23) and the continuous embedding H2 ,→ L ∞, we know that (h, h Γ) ∈ L∞(0 , T ; L∞).Next, consider the ODE problems 

(

γ∂ tU + k−1fσ(U ) = H, U (0) = (1 − σ)(1 − δ0),

(

γ∂ tV + k−1fσ(V ) = −H, V (0) = (1 − σ)( −1 + δ0), (A.25) where 

H := max {∥ h∥L∞(Ω) , ∥hΓ∥L∞(Γ) }.

We note that due to (A.20) and (A.23), H may depend on γ and σ. It is straightforward to check that there exist two unique solutions U, V ∈ C([0 , T ]) with ∂tU , ∂tV ∈ L∞(0 , T ) to the two ODE systems in (A.25), respectively. Define 

δ := 1 − max 1 − δ0, f −10 (k∥H∥L∞(0 ,T )), −f −10 (−k∥H∥L∞(0 ,T )) ,M := (1 − σ)(1 − δ).

Since δ0 > 0 and f −10 (R) = ( −1, 1) , it follows that δ > 0. It is easy to check that −M is a subsolution for the ODE involving V , and M is a supersolution for the ODE involving U . Moreover, since 0 < δ ≤ δ0, we further have −M ≤ V (0) ≤ U (0) ≤ M . Hence, a simple comparison argument entails that 

−M ≤ V (t) ≤ U (t) ≤ M for all t ∈ [0 , T ].

Next, we show that 

V (t) ≤ φ(x, t ) ≤ U (t) for all (x, t ) ∈ QT , V (t) ≤ ψ(x, t ) ≤ U (t) for all (x, t ) ∈ ΣT .

γ∂ twΓ − ∆τ ψ + ∂nφ + k−1fσ(ψ) − k−1fσ(U ) = hΓ − H a.e. on ΣT , (A.26c) 

w(0) = φ0 − (1 − σ)(1 − δ0) a.e. in Ω, (A.26d) 

wΓ(0) = ψ0 − (1 − σ)(1 − δ0) a.e. on Γ. (A.26e) Multiplying (A.26a) by w+ = max {φ − U, 0} and integrating over Ω, multiplying (A.26c) by w+Γ = max {ψ −

φ(x, t ) ≤ U (t) ≤ M in QT , ψ(x, t ) ≤ U (t) ≤ M on ΣT .

Similarly, we can conclude 

φ(x, t ) ≥ V (t) ≥ − M in QT , ψ(x, t ) ≥ V (t) ≥ − M on ΣT .

This verifies the strict separation property (A.6). As a consequence, we have 

(k−1fσ(φ), k −1fσ(ψ)) ∈ L∞(0 , T ; H1).

Applying the elliptic regularity theory for bulk-surface elliptic systems (see, e.g., [41, Theorem 3.3]) to system (A.21) (or (A.24), respectively), we finally arrive at (φ, ψ ) ∈ L2(0 , T ; H3).

Finally, applying the elliptic regularity theory for bulk-surface elliptic systems (see, e.g., [41, Theorem 3.3]) to the system (A.21) (resp. (A.24)), we find (φ, ψ ) ∈ L∞(0 , T ; H3). Thus, the proof of Theorem A.1 is complete.
